Thus, in Eq. (4.26) we are reducing the computation of the partition function of the original 2d model to the analysis of the partition function of an effective one-dimensional model; the effective potential of this one-dimensional model corresponds to the “projection” of the original kernels, on the two edges. The effective potential is analytic for |λ ||λ| small enough; furthermore, from the bound (4.20) and the assumptions on the edge modes (2.19), we have:4.29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Big | \text {d}_{k_{0}}^{n_{0}} \text {d}_{k_{1}}^{n_{1}} V_{n;\omega ,\omega '}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{k}) \Big | \le C_{n_{0},n_{1}} |\lambda | e^{-c|n|} e^{-{\tilde{c}}\delta _{\omega ,-\omega '}L_{2}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|dk00dk11Vn;ω,ω′(1)(k)|≤Cn0,n1|λ|e-c|n|e-c~δω,-ω′L2.In particular, we see that the scattering between the different edges is exponentially suppressed, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{2}$$\end{document}L2 large. To conclude the paragraph, it is useful to write the first order contribution to the effective potential. It is:4.30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} V_{n;\omega ,\omega '}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{k})= -\lambda \sum _{x_{2},\sigma } \overline{\xi ^{\omega }_{\sigma }(k_{1}; x_{2})} \xi ^{\omega '}_{\sigma }(k_{1} + n\alpha ; y_{2}) \hat{\varphi }_{-n}(x_{2})+\mathrm O(\lambda ^2). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Vn;ω,ω′(1)(k)=-λ∑x2,σξσω(k1;x2)¯ξσ′(k1+nα;y2)φ^-n(x2)+O(λ2).Thus, we see that, already at first order in λ λ, the two edge modes are coupled by the disorder. Also, we conclude by observing that the analogue of (4.21) holds true, as a consequence of the symmetry property (4.7):4.31\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \overline{V_{n;\omega ,\omega '}^{(1)}((k_{0}, k_{1}))} = V_{-n;\omega ',\omega }^{(1)}((-k_{0}, k_{1} + n\alpha )). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Vn;ω,ω′(1)((k0,k1))¯=V-n;ω′,ω(1)((-k0,k1+nα)).

Integration of the First Scale

Shift of the Fermi points. We now set up the multiscale integration of the one-dimensional field. In order to do this, it is convenient to allow for a small shift of the Fermi points. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi ^{(\le 1)}_{\omega }({\boldsymbol{k}}):= \chi (k_{0}) \chi (\varepsilon _\omega (k_1)-\mu )$$\end{document}χω(≤1)(k):=χ(k0)χ(εω(k1)-μ). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _{\omega }({\boldsymbol{k}}) = \nu _{\omega } \chi ^{(\le 1)}_{\omega }({\boldsymbol{k}})$$\end{document}νω(k)=νωχω(≤1)(k) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _{\omega }$$\end{document}νω real-valued, to be determined. This quantity plays the role of counterterm in our RG analysis; for the moment, its precise numerical value will not be important, and we will only assume that it satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\nu _{\omega }| \le C|\lambda |$$\end{document}|νω|≤C|λ|. Thus, we now have that the denominator of the covariance is vanishing in correspondence with a slightly different point, to be denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{k}}_{F}^{\omega }(\lambda ) = \big (0, k_{F}^{\omega }(\lambda )\big )$$\end{document}kFω(λ)=(0,kFω(λ)), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k^{\omega }_{F}(\lambda )$$\end{document}kFω(λ) solves:4.37\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \varepsilon _{\omega }\big (k^{\omega }_{F}(\lambda )\big ) - \mu + \nu _{\omega } = 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}εω(kFω(λ))-μ+νω=0.Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\nu _{\omega }|\le C|\lambda |$$\end{document}|νω|≤C|λ|, we also have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$| k^{\omega }_{F}(\lambda ) - k_{F}^{\omega } | \le K|\lambda |$$\end{document}|kFω(λ)-kFω|≤K|λ|. The new Fermi point is thus parametrized by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _{\omega }$$\end{document}νω, and the final value of the renormalized Fermi points will be fixed after choosing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _{\omega }$$\end{document}νω as in Proposition (4.9). Observe that Eq. (4.37) might not have a solution in the discrete Brillouin zone \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}_{L}$$\end{document}DL. With a slight abuse of notation, we shall use the notation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k^{\omega }_{F}(\lambda )$$\end{document}kFω(λ) to denote the best lattice approximation of the solution of (4.37), for which the identity (4.37) holds with 0 in the right-hand side replaced by a quantity bounded by C/L. This offset will not play any role in what follows.

Next, we center the momenta around the two new Fermi points. We rewrite any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{k}}$$\end{document}k in the support of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{g}}^{(\le 1)}_{\omega }({\boldsymbol{k}})$$\end{document}g~ω(≤1)(k) as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{k}} = {\boldsymbol{q}} + {\boldsymbol{k}}_{F}^{\omega }(\lambda )$$\end{document}k=q+kFω(λ), with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert {\boldsymbol{q}} \Vert \le C\delta $$\end{document}‖q‖≤Cδ. With a slight abuse of notation, we shall set:4.38\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \psi ^{(\le 1)\pm }_{{\boldsymbol{q}} + {\boldsymbol{k}_{F}^{\omega }}(\lambda ), \omega } \equiv \psi ^{(\le 1)\pm }_{{\boldsymbol{q}}, \omega }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψq+kFω(λ),ω(≤1)±≡ψq,ω(≤1)±.Whevener it does not create ambiguities, in what follows we may drop the λ λ-dependence of the shifted Fermi points.

In order to iterate this integration to smaller scales, and to ensure a positive radius of convergence in λ λ, we will have to introduce a suitable localization and renormalization procedure.

Iterative Integration

In this section we shall discuss the integration of the smaller scales. To do so, we will have to introduce a suitable localization and renormalization procedure. This is the content of the next proposition.

Proposition 4.2

(Multiscale construction of the effective potential). Under the same assumption of Theorem 3.1, the following holds. Remark 4.3

(i)The proof of the proposition actually yields an explicit construction of the objects appearing in Proposition 4.2 via a convergent expansion.(ii)The above result shows that, in a quantitative sense, the low-energy renormalized covariance of the Grassmann field theory agrees with the one of massless, relativistic fermions in 1+11+1 dimensions (the term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_{\omega }(q_{1})$$\end{document}rω(q1) takes into account the nonlinearity of the bands), exposed to a suitable effective potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}^{(h)}$$\end{document}V(h), which breaks translation-invariance, and which gets weaker on smaller scales.

The above result shows that, in a quantitative sense, the low-energy renormalized covariance of the Grassmann field theory agrees with the one of massless, relativistic fermions in 1+11+1 dimensions (the term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_{\omega }(q_{1})$$\end{document}rω(q1) takes into account the nonlinearity of the bands), exposed to a suitable effective potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}^{(h)}$$\end{document}V(h), which breaks translation-invariance, and which gets weaker on smaller scales.

The rest of Sect. 4.2 will be devoted to the proof of Proposition 4.2. We will proceed in an inductive fashion. Observe that the claims of the Proposition are true on scale 0, by the discussion of Sects. 4.2.1, 4.2.2.

Localization and renormalization. In order to integrate the scale h, we will renormalize the Gaussian integration, by reabsorbing into the covariance the dangerous contributions to the effective potential. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{0}}^{+}_{\beta }:= (\pi /\beta , 0)$$\end{document}0β+:=(π/β,0), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{0}}^{-}_{\beta }:= (-\pi /\beta , 0)$$\end{document}0β-:=(-π/β,0) and let, for an arbitrary function F:4.55\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F({\boldsymbol{0}}_{\beta }):= \frac{1}{2} \sum _{\alpha = \pm } F({\boldsymbol{0}}^{\alpha }_{\beta }). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F(0β):=12∑α=±F(0βα).We are now ready to introduce the notion of localization.

Definition 4.4

(Localization operator). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^{(h)}_{n;\omega ,\omega '}({\boldsymbol{q}})$$\end{document}Vn;ω,ω′(h)(q) be a coefficient of the effective potential (4.51). We define:4.56\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathfrak L V^{(h)}_{n;\omega ,\omega '}({\boldsymbol{q}}):= \delta _{n,0} \delta _{\omega ,\omega '}\big (V^{(h)}_{n;\omega ,\omega '}({\boldsymbol{0}}_{\beta }) + q_{0} \text {d}_{q_{0}}\! V^{(h)}_{n;\omega ,\omega '}({\boldsymbol{0}}_{\beta }) + q_{1} \text {d}_{q_{1}}\! V^{(h)}_{n;\omega ,\omega '}({\boldsymbol{0}}_{\beta })\big ). \nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}LVn;ω,ω′(h)(q):=δn,0δω,ω′(Vn;ω,ω′(h)(0β)+q0dq0Vn;ω,ω′(h)(0β)+q1dq1Vn;ω,ω′(h)(0β)).The action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {L}$$\end{document}L is extended to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}^{(h)}$$\end{document}V(h) by linearity. Furthermore, we define:4.57\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathfrak RV^{(h)}_{n;\omega ,\omega '}({\boldsymbol{q}}):= V^{(h)}_{n;\omega ,\omega '}({\boldsymbol{q}}) - \mathfrak L V^{(h)}_{n;\omega ,\omega '}({\boldsymbol{q}}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}RVn;ω,ω′(h)(q):=Vn;ω,ω′(h)(q)-LVn;ω,ω′(h)(q).We shall also set:4.58\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathfrak L_{0} V^{(h)}_{n;\omega ,\omega }({\boldsymbol{q}}):= & \delta _{n,0} V^{(h)}_{0;\omega ,\omega }({\boldsymbol{0}}_{\beta }),\quad \nonumber \\ \mathfrak L_{1} V^{(h)}_{n;\omega ,\omega }({\boldsymbol{q}}):= & \delta _{n,0}\big ( q_{0} \text {d}_{q_{0}}\! Remark 4.5

(i)The operator \mathfrak LL is called the localization operator, and its role is to extract from the effective potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}^{(h)}$$\end{document}V(h) the relevant and marginal terms in the renormalization group sense; these terms require a separate discussion. Ultimately, they will be controlled by a suitable choice of the parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _{\omega }$$\end{document}νω, entering the definition of shifted chemical potentials.(ii)The operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak L_{0}$$\end{document}L0 isolates the relevant terms in the renormalization group sense, while the operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak L_{1}$$\end{document}L1 isolates the marginal terms in the renormalization group sense. The naive dimensional estimate for the former diverges exponentially in |h|, while the naive dimensional estimate for the latter diverge linearly in |h|. Observe that we are not defining a localization operator on the “off-diagonal terms”, that is the terms involving different \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega ,\omega '$$\end{document}ω,ω′ labels. The beta function. Let us now use the localization operator to rewrite the argument of the Grassmann integral in a way which is more convenient for setting up an iterative integration. We have:4.60\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \int \mathbb {P}_{(\le h)} (d\psi ^{(\le h)}) \,e^{\mathcal {V}^{(h)}(\psi ^{(\le h)})}&= \int \mathbb {P}_{(\le h)} (d\psi ^{(\le h)})\, e^{(\mathfrak L_{0} + \mathfrak L_{1} + \mathfrak R) \mathcal {V}^{(h)}(\psi ^{(\le h)})} \\&= {\tilde{z}}_{h} \int \widetilde{\mathbb {P}}_{(\le h)} (d\psi ^{(\le h)})\, e^{(\mathfrak L_{0} + \mathfrak R) \mathcal {V}^{(h)}(\psi ^{(\le h)})}, \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫P(≤h)(dψ(≤h))eV(h)(ψ(≤h))=∫P(≤h)(dψ(≤h))e(L0+L1+R)V(h)(ψ(≤h))=z~h∫P~(≤h)(dψ(≤h))e(L0+R)V(h)(ψ(≤h)),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\mathbb {P}}_{(\le h)}$$\end{document}P~(≤h) is a new Grassmann Gaussian integration, whose covariance is renormalized by including \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak L_{1} \mathcal {V}^{(h)}$$\end{document}L1V(h) into its definition. The constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{z}}_{h}$$\end{document}z~h takes into account the change of normalization of the integral, and it is bounded as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|{\tilde{z}}_{h}| \le \text {exp}(\beta L_{1} C|\lambda | \gamma ^{h})$$\end{document}|z~h|≤exp(βL1C|λ|γh). Let us discuss the structure of the new covariance. To this end, we define:4.61\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} -\text {d}_{q_{0}}\! Remark 4.6

Thus, we can allow for a cylinder with finite width \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{2}$$\end{document}L2, provided that the width grows at least linearly in the inverse temperature β β. In this range of parameters, the two edge modes living on the two boundaries of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _{L}$$\end{document}ΛL are only weakly correlated.

In order to prove the inductive assumption stated at the beginning of this section, and to actually derive an explicit representation for the effective potential at all scales, we will introduce a graphical representation for the effective potential at a given scale in terms of a hierarchy of clusters. This will be the goal of the next section.

Chain Expansion

The goal of this section will be to set up a graphical representation for the effective potential at all scales, which will be useful to prove estimates for the kernels of the potential, and in particular to prove Eq. (4.52). This representation has been introduced in [8] and it has been used in many subsequent works. It is similar to the one used recently in [23]. The main difference here is the presence of two Fermi points instead of one, corresponding to the two edge modes of the system. However, thanks to the localization properties of the edge modes, the fields corresponding to the two Fermi points will be essentially independent, provided \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{2}$$\end{document}L2 is chosen large enough. Since the structure of the expansion will be used in the rest of the paper, we prefer to rederive it, rather than referring to previous papers and stating the differences.

Being the Grassmann theory quadratic in the fermions, the graphical representation will involve chain graphs, as in Sect. 4.1. We will express the kernel of the effective potential on scale h-1h-1 as:4.75\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} V^{(h-1)}_{n;\omega ,\omega '}({\boldsymbol{q}}) = \sum _{s \ge 1} \bigg (\frac{1}{s!} \sum _{\theta \in {\mathscr {T}}_{n;s, \omega ,\omega '}^{(h)}}\theta ({\boldsymbol{q}})\bigg ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Vn;ω,ω′(h-1)(q)=∑s≥1(1s!∑θ∈Tn;s,ω,ω′(h)θ(q)),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathscr {T}}_{n;s, \omega ,\omega '}^{(h)}$$\end{document}Tn;s,ω,ω′(h) is the set of labelled chains on scale h, to be defined after Eq. (4.83) below, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ({\boldsymbol{q}})$$\end{document}θ(q) is the value associated with each chain with external quasi-momentum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{q}}$$\end{document}q. Let us explain how the graphs in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathscr {T}}_{n;s, \omega ,\omega '}^{(h)}$$\end{document}Tn;s,ω,ω′(h) are defined. After the integration of the scale h, the effective potential is expressed as a sum of chain graphs similarly to what we discussed in (4.18). The definition of the values of the chains proceeds as after (4.18), with the following differences. To every node v, v = 1,… , sv=1,…,s, we attach a frequency label \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_{v}$$\end{document}nv, and two labels, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _{v}$$\end{document}ωv and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega '_{v}$$\end{document}ωv′, associated respectively with the outgoing and the incoming line to the node; we impose the constraint \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega '_{s} = \omega '$$\end{document}ωs′=ω′ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _{1} = \omega $$\end{document}ω1=ω. The edge outgoing from a node v is associated with a single scale propagator, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g^{(h)}_{\omega _{v}}\big ({\boldsymbol{q}} + {\boldsymbol{n}}(v)\big )$$\end{document}gv(h)(q+n(v)), where:4.76\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\boldsymbol{n}}(v):= \sum _{v'\le v} \Big (n_{v'} {\boldsymbol{\alpha }} - {\boldsymbol{k}}_{F}^{\omega _{v}} + {\boldsymbol{k}}_{F}^{\omega '_{v}}\Big ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}n(v):=∑v′≤v(nv′α-kFv+kFωv′)has the interpretation of quasi-momentum outgoing a vertex v. The sum over all the quasi-momentum shifts satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{n}}(v) = n {\boldsymbol{\alpha }} - \delta _{n\ne 0}({\boldsymbol{k}}_{F}^{\omega }(\lambda ) - {\boldsymbol{k}}_{F}^{\omega '}(\lambda ))$$\end{document}n(v)=nα-δn≠0(kFω(λ)-kF′(λ)). To every node of the chain graph we associate:4.77\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} N^{(h)}_{n_{v}; \omega _{v}, \omega '_{v}}\big ({\boldsymbol{q}} + {\boldsymbol{n}}'(v)\big ):= (\mathfrak L_{0} + \mathfrak R) V^{(h)}_{n_{v};\omega _{v},\omega '_{v}}\big ({\boldsymbol{q}} + {\boldsymbol{n}}'(v)\big ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Nnv;ωv,ωv′(h)(q+n′(v)):=(L0+R)Vnv;ωv,ωv′(h)(q+n′(v))where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{n}}'(v) = {\boldsymbol{n}}(v-1)$$\end{document}n′(v)=n(v-1): the momentum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{q}} + {\boldsymbol{n}}'(v)$$\end{document}q+n′(v) has the interpretation of incoming quasi-momentum in the node v. Thus, we can represent \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^{(h-1)}_{n;\omega ,\omega '}$$\end{document}Vn;ω,ω′(h-1) as the sum of chain graphs with the following values (compare with (4.19)):4.78\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \theta ({\boldsymbol{q}}) = \Big (\prod _{v=1}^s N^{(h)}_{n_{v}; \omega _{v}, \omega '_{v}}\big ({\boldsymbol{q}} + {\boldsymbol{n}}'(v)\big ) \Big ) \Big (\prod _{v=1}^{s-1}g^{(h)}_{\omega _{v}}\big ({\boldsymbol{q}} + {\boldsymbol{n}}(v)\big )\Big ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}θ(q)=(∏v=1sNnv;ωv,ωv′(h)(q+n′(v)))(∏v=1s-1gv(h)(q+n(v))).We can obtain a more explicit expression combining the definition (4.77) with the identity (4.78). (v)A cluster T is a connected segment contained in the chain θ θ, so that the incoming line and the outgoing line of T have scale labels strictly smaller than the scales contained in the cluster. We use the convention that clusters must contain at least one edge: that is, nodes are not clusters. A cluster T is a connected segment contained in the chain θ θ, so that the incoming line and the outgoing line of T have scale labels strictly smaller than the scales contained in the cluster. We use the convention that clusters must contain at least one edge: that is, nodes are not clusters. We denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}(\theta )$$\end{document}C(θ) the set of clusters strictly contained in θ θ and by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_{0}(\theta )$$\end{document}C0(θ) the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}(\theta ) \cup \theta $$\end{document}C(θ)∪θ. (vi)A cluster is resonant if the incoming and the outgoing momenta of T are equal, and if the quasi-particle labels of the incoming and of the outgoing lines are equal. Otherwise, the cluster is called non-resonant. We denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_{\text {r}}(\theta )$$\end{document}Cr(θ) the set of resonant clusters strictly contained in θ θ, and by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_{\text {nr}}(\theta )$$\end{document}Cnr(θ) the set of non-resonant clusters strictly contained in θ θ. A cluster T'⊂ TT′⊂T is maximal if it is not contained into any other subcluster of T. Similarly, a node v∈ Tv∈T is called maximal if it is not contained into any other subcluster of T. A cluster is resonant if the incoming and the outgoing momenta of T are equal, and if the quasi-particle labels of the incoming and of the outgoing lines are equal. Otherwise, the cluster is called non-resonant. An example of a chain graph with 8 nodes and its clusters structure. For simplicity we omitted the ω ω labels and all arguments excepts a few ones. Each clusters is represented by a curly bracket

The next proposition is the main technical result of the section.

Proposition 4.7

(Bound for chain graphs). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in {\mathscr {T}}_{n;s, \omega ,\omega '}^{(h)}$$\end{document}θ∈Tn;s,ω,ω′(h). Then, assuming the validity of (4.70) the following estimate holds true:4.84\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} | \text {d}_{0}^{n_{0}} \text {d}_{1}^{n_{1}} \theta ({\boldsymbol{q}}) |&\le C_{n_{0},n_{1}}\gamma ^{h(1 - n_{0} - n_{1})} e^{- \varepsilon (\theta )2^{h} C \gamma ^{-h / \tau }} K^{|\mathscr {N}(\theta )|} |\lambda |^{|\mathscr {N}_{\text {nr}}(\theta )|} \Big ( \prod _{v\in \mathscr {N}(\theta )} e^{-\frac{c}{2}|n_{v}|} \Big ) \\&\quad \cdot \Big ( \prod _{v\in \mathscr {N}_{\text {r}}(\theta )} |\nu _{\omega _{v},h_{v}}| \Big )\Big (\prod _{v\in \mathscr {N}_{\text {nr}}(\theta )} e^{- \frac{{\tilde{c}}}{4} \delta _{\omega _{v},-\omega _{v}} L_{2}} \Big ) \Big (\prod _{T \in \mathscr {C}(\theta ) \cup \mathscr {N}_{\text {nr}}(\theta )} \gamma ^{ (h^{\text {ext}}_{T} - h_{T})}\Big ) \end{aligned}\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|d00d11θ(q)|≤Cn0,n1γh(1-n0-n1)e-ε(θ)2hCγ-h/τK|N(θ)||λ||Nnr(θ)|(∏v∈N(θ)e-c2|nv|)·(∏v∈Nr(θ)|νωv,hv|)(∏v∈Nnr(θ)e-c~4δωv,-ωvL2)(∏T∈C(θ)∪Nnr(θ)γ(hText-hT))where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon (\theta ) = 1$$\end{document}ε(θ)=1 if the cluster is non-resonant and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon (\theta ) = 0$$\end{document}ε(θ)=0 otherwise.

The bound (4.84) is summable over the scale labels, and over the frequency labels. In fact:4.85\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{\{n_{v}\}: \sum _{v} n_{v} = n}\Big ( \prod _{v\in \mathscr {N}(\theta )} e^{-\frac{c}{2}|n_{v}|}\Big )\le & C^{|\mathscr {N}(\theta )|} e^{-\frac{c}{4} |n|} \quad \text {and}\quad \nonumber \\ \sum _{\{h_{T}\}: h^{\text {ext}}_{\theta } = h}\Big (\prod _{T \in \mathscr {C}(\theta )} \gamma ^{ (h^{\text {ext}}_{T} - h_{T})}\Big )\le & 1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑{nv}:∑vnv=n(∏v∈N(θ)e-c2|nv|)≤C|N(θ)|e-c4|n|and∑{hT}:hθext=h(∏T∈C(θ)γ(hText-hT))≤1.Concerning the combinatorics for a given cluster T, the number of chain graphs obtained connecting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_{T}$$\end{document}sT maximal clusters or nodes is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_{T}!$$\end{document}sT!. On the other hand, following the iterative construction of the chain graphs discussed starting from (4.75), we see that the value of the resulting graphs is accompanied by a prefactor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\prod _{T \in \mathscr {C}_{0}(\theta )} (1/s_{T}!)$$\end{document}∏T∈C0(θ)(1/sT!). Thus, the overall combinatorial factor is 1. In conclusion, from (4.75) we get, up to a redefinition of the overall constant and for |λ ||λ| small enough:4.86\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} | \text {d}_{0}^{n_{0}} \text {d}_{1}^{n_{1}} V^{(h-1)}_{n;\omega ,\omega '}({\boldsymbol{q}})| \le C_{n_{0},n_{1}}|\lambda | \gamma ^{(h-1)(1 - n_{0} - n_{1})} e^{-\frac{c}{4} |n|} e^{- \frac{{\tilde{c}}}{4} \delta _{\omega ,-\omega '} L_{2}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|d00d11Vn;ω,ω′(h-1)(q)|≤Cn0,n1|λ|γ(h-1)(1-n0-n1)e-c4|n|e-c~4δω,-ω′L2,which is the claimed bound (4.52) on scale h-1h-1.

Proof of Proposition 4.7

To begin, it is useful to first introduce the unrenormalized value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _{\text {nr}}$$\end{document}θnr, as follows:4.87\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \theta _{\text {nr}}({\boldsymbol{q}}) = \Big (\prod _{v \in \mathscr {N}_{\text {r}}(\theta )} V_{v}\Big ) \Big (\prod _{v\in \mathscr {N}_{\text {nr}}(\theta )} {\mathfrak R}V^{(0)}_{n_{v}; \omega _{v},\omega '_{v}}\big ({\boldsymbol{q}} + {\boldsymbol{n}}'(v)\big ) \Big ) \Big (\prod _{e\in \mathscr {E}(\theta )} g^{(h_{e})}_{\omega _{e}}\big ({\boldsymbol{q}} + \tilde{\boldsymbol{n}}(e)\big )\Big ), \nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}θnr(q)=(∏v∈Nr(θ)Vv)(∏v∈Nnr(θ)RVnv;ωv,ωv′(0)(q+n′(v)))(∏e∈E(θ)ge(he)(q+n~(e))),where if e = (v, v+1)e=(v,v+1), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\boldsymbol{n}}(e) = {\boldsymbol{n}}(v)$$\end{document}n~(e)=n(v). This expression is generated iterating (4.81) neglecting the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak R}$$\end{document}R operators acting on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _{h+1}$$\end{document}θh+1. Let us first derive an estimate for the unrenormalized value, and then discuss the actual estimate for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ({\boldsymbol{q}})$$\end{document}θ(q), taking into account the action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak R}$$\end{document}R.

Assume that the estimates for the running coupling constants (4.70) hold true on scales greater or equal than h, and recall the bound (4.81) for the single-scale propagator and the bound (4.45) for the kernels on scale 0. Then, we have:4.88\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} | \theta _{\text {nr}}({\boldsymbol{q}}) | \le \Big (\prod _{v \in \mathscr {N}_{\text {r}}(\theta )} \gamma ^{h_{v}} |\nu _{\omega _{v},h_{v}}| \Big ) \Big (\prod _{v\in \mathscr {N}_{\text {nr}}(\theta )} C|\lambda | e^{-c|n_{v}|} e^{-\frac{{\tilde{c}}}{2} \delta _{\omega _{v},-\omega _{v}} L_{2}} \Big ) \Big (\prod _{e\in \mathscr {E}(\theta )} C\gamma ^{-h_{e}}\Big ). \nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|θnr(q)|≤(∏v∈Nr(θ)γhv|νωv,hv|)(∏v∈Nnr(θ)C|λ|e-c|nv|e-c~2δωv,-ωvL2)(∏e∈E(θ)Cγ-he).The sum over all chains implies a sum over all scale labels. Due to the last factor in Eq. (4.88), it is clear that the bound (4.88) cannot be used to prove the bound (4.52) for the effective potential on scale h.

In order to improve the situation, we have to take into account the action of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak R}$$\end{document}R operator in (4.81), on a general \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _{h+1}({\boldsymbol{q}})$$\end{document}θh+1(q). The action is nontrivial only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _{h+1}({\boldsymbol{q}})$$\end{document}θh+1(q) is associated with a resonant cluster. To begin, observe that the estimate (4.88) can be rewritten as:4.89\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} | \theta _{\text {nr}}({\boldsymbol{q}}) |\le & \Big (\prod _{v \in \mathscr {N}_{\text {r}}(\theta )} \gamma ^{h_{v}} |\nu _{\omega _{v},h_{v}}| \Big ) \Big (\prod _{v\in \mathscr {N}_{\text {nr}}(\theta )} C|\lambda | e^{-c|n_{v}|} e^{-\frac{{\tilde{c}}}{2} \delta _{\omega _{v},-\omega _{v}} L_{2}} \Big ) \nonumber \\ & \cdot \Big (\prod _{T\in \mathscr {C}_{0}(\theta )} \gamma ^{-h_{T} (s_{T} - h_{T})}$$\end{document}γ2(hText-hT) in the estimates, see e.g. [8, 24]: the factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ^{2 h^{\text {ext}}_{T}}$$\end{document}γ2hText is the estimate for the external momenta to the cluster, while the factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ^{-2 h_{T}}$$\end{document}γ-2hT takes into account the action of the second derivative. Thus, the action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak R}$$\end{document}R on all resonant clusters introduces the following factor in the bound for the chain:4.90\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \prod _{T \in \mathscr {C}_{\text {r}}(\theta )} \gamma ^{2(h^{\text {ext}}_{T} - To better understand the right-hand side of (4.91), let us write:4.92\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \prod _{T\in \mathscr {C}_{0}(\theta )} \gamma ^{-h_{T} (s_{T} - 1)} = \Big (\prod _{T\in \mathscr {C}_{0}(\theta )} \gamma ^{-h_{T} (s^{\text {r}}_{T} - 1)}\Big ) \Big (\prod _{T\in \mathscr {C}_{0}(\theta )} \gamma ^{-h_{T} s^{\text {nr}}_{T}}\Big ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∏T∈C0(θ)γ-hT(sT-1)=(∏T∈C0(θ)γ-hT(sTr-1))(∏T∈C0(θ)γ-hTsTnr),where we recall that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_{T}^{\text {r}}$$\end{document}sTr is the number of maximal resonant clusters or vertices contained in T, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_{T}^{\text {nr}}$$\end{document}sTnr is the number of non-resonant maximal clusters or vertices contained in T. We will control the first factor in the right-hand side of (4.92) using part of the last factor in (4.91). Calling \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s^{\text {c,r}}_{T}$$\end{document}sTc,r the number of maximal resonant clusters contained in T:4.93\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \Big (\prod _{T\in \mathscr {C}_{0}(\theta )} \gamma ^{-h_{T} (s^{\text {r}}_{T} - Remark 4.8

The above estimate makes it clear why the localization operation is defined up to order 1 in the Taylor expansion in the momenta, recall Definition 4.4: it is essential that the prefactor of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(h^{\text {ext}}_{T} - h_{T})$$\end{document}(hText-hT) in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ^{2 (h^{\text {ext}}_{T} - h_{T})}$$\end{document}γ2(hText-hT) in the bound (4.91) is bigger than 1, in order to control the sum over the scale labels of the resonant clusters, as we did above.

We are left with discussing the dependence on the scale labels associated with non-resonant clusters. Here, a key role will be played by the approximate Diophantine property (2.9); it will allow us to use part of the second factor in the second line of (4.94) to overcompensate the bad third factor in (4.94). Let us define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {N}_{\text {nr}}^{\text {d}}(\theta )$$\end{document}Nnrd(θ) as the set of diagonal non-resonant nodes, that is with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _{v} = \omega '_{v}$$\end{document}ωv=ωv′ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_{v}\ne 0$$\end{document}nv≠0, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {N}_{\text {nr}}^{\text {od}}(\theta )$$\end{document}Nnrod(θ) as the set of off-diagonal non-resonant nodes, that is with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _{v} \ne \omega '_{v}$$\end{document}ωv≠ωv′. We start by writing:4.95\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Big (\prod _{v\in \mathscr {N}_{\text {nr}}^{\text {od}}(\theta )}e^{-\frac{{\tilde{c}}}{4} L_{2}}\Big ) \Big (\prod _{v\in \mathscr {N}^{\text {d}}_{\text {nr}}(\theta )} e^{-\frac{c}{2}|n_{v}|}\Big ) = \prod _{v\in \mathscr {N}_{\text {nr}}(\theta )} e^{-a_{v}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(∏v∈Nnrod(θ)e-c~4L2)(∏v∈Nnrd(θ)e-c2|nv|)=∏v∈Nnr(θ)e-avwith4.96\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} a_{v}:= (c/2) |n_{v}| \quad \text {if } v\in \mathscr {N}_{\text {nr}}^{\text {d}}(\theta ) \quad \text {and}\quad a_{v} = ({\tilde{c}} /4) L_{2}\quad \text {if } v\in \mathscr {N}_{\text {nr}}^{\text {od}}(\theta ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}av:=(c/2)|nv|ifv∈Nnrd(θ)andav=(c~/4)L2ifv∈Nnrod(θ).Then,4.97\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \prod _{v\in \mathscr {N}_{\text {nr}}(\theta )} e^{-a_{v}}&= \prod _{v\in \mathscr {N}_{\text {nr}}(\theta )} \prod _{k = -\infty }^{0} e^{-2^{k} a_{v}} \\&\le \prod _{k = -\infty }^{0} \prod _{\begin{array}{c} v\in \mathscr {N}_{\text {nr}}(\theta ) \\ \text {s.t.}\,v\in T\, \text {with}\, h^{\text {ext}}_{T} = k \end{array}} e^{-2^{h^{\text {ext}}_{T}} \!\! a_{v} } \le \prod _{T \in \theta \cup \mathscr {C}_{\text {nr}}(\theta ) \cup \mathscr {N}_{\text {nr}}(\theta )} e^{-2^{h^{\text {ext}}_{T}}\!\! a_{T}} \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∏v∈Nnr(θ)e-av=∏v∈Nnr(θ)∏k=-∞0e-2kav≤∏k=-∞0∏v∈Nnr(θ)s.t.v∈TwithhText=ke-2hTextav≤∏T∈θ∪Cnr(θ)∪Nnr(θ)e-2hTextaTwhere we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{T}:= \sum _{v\in T} a_{v}$$\end{document}aT:=∑v∈Tav. In the last inequality of (4.97), the product runs over clusters and endpoints, and with a slight abuse of notation we use the symbol T to denote the elements of both sets. The presence of the non-resonant endpoints in the last product is due to the contributions of the clusters T with external scale zero, which are by definition endpoints. Given T in the last product in (4.97), suppose that T contains at least one non-resonant off-diagonal node. Then:4.98\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} e^{-2^{h^{\text {ext}}_{T}}a_{T}} \le e^{-2^{h^{\text {ext}}_{T}} \frac{{\tilde{c}}}{4} L_{2}} \le e^{-2^{h^{\text {ext}}_{T}} {\tilde{C}} \gamma ^{-h^{\text {ext}}_{T}}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}e-2hTextaT≤e-2hTextc~4L2≤e-2hTextC~γ-hTextwhere we used that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa L_{2} \ge \beta \ge C\gamma ^{-h^{\text {ext}}_{T}}$$\end{document}κL2≥β≥Cγ-hText. The Flow of the Running Coupling Constants

To conclude the proof of Proposition 4.2, in this section we will control the flow of the running coupling constants. In particular, our goal will be to show the bounds (4.70) on all scales greater or equal than h. This is the content of the next proposition.

Proposition 4.9

(The flow of the beta function). There exists a choice of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _{\omega } = O(\lambda )$$\end{document}νω=O(λ) in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R such that the estimates (4.70) hold true on all scales.

Proof

We will show how to prove the bounds for the marginal terms, assuming the validity of the bound for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _{h,\omega }$$\end{document}νh,ω, and the validity of the same estimates on scales strictly greater than h. The proof of the bound for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _{h,\omega }$$\end{document}νh,ω can be done via a standard fixed point argument which has been carried out in many previous papers, see e.g. Section 6.4.2 of [26] for a recent application (in a more difficult context). Recall the flow equation (4.64)–(4.68); the beta function can be represented in terms of chain graphs, as follows:4.108\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \beta ^{v}_{\nu ,\omega ,h+1} = i^{\delta _{\nu },0}\sum _{s \ge 1} \frac{1}{s!} \sum _{\theta \in {\mathscr {T}}_{n;s, \omega ,\omega }^{(h+1)}} \text {d}_{\nu } \theta ({\boldsymbol{0}}_{\beta }). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}βν,ω,h+1v=iδν,0∑s≥11s!∑θ∈Tn;s,ω,ω(h+1)dνθ(0β).The argument of the sum could be estimated with (4.84). This however would produce a bound that is not summable in h, and it would not allow to check the desired estimates on scale h. To obtain a better estimate, we observe that if all nodes of the chain θ θ contributing to the beta function are resonant, i.e. associated with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_{v} = 0$$\end{document}nv=0, then the quasi-momentum flowing on every propagator on the chain is either \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{0}}_{\beta }^{+}$$\end{document}0β+ or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{0}}_{\beta }^{-}$$\end{document}0β-. Since these quasi-momenta do not belong to the support of the propagators on scales h+1h+1 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h\ge h_{\beta }$$\end{document}h≥hβ, the corresponding chain graph is vanishing.

Thus, all the chain graphs contributing to the beta function must have at least one non-resonant node. In fact, since the outgoing and the incoming momentum of the whole chain have to be the same, there must be at least two non-resonant nodes. Also, the chain graphs contributing to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{v}_{\nu ,\omega ,h+1}$$\end{document}βν,ω,h+1v must have at least one propagator on scale h+1h+1; let us denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(v_{*}, v_{*}+1)$$\end{document}(v∗,v∗+1) the two nodes connected by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g^{(h+1)}_{\omega _{v_{*}}}$$\end{document}gωv∗(h+1). By the Kirchhoff rule, the momentum flowing on this propagator is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{n}}(v_{*})$$\end{document}n(v∗), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{n}}(v_{*}) \ne 0$$\end{document}n(v∗)≠0 otherwise the propagator is vanishing, by the support of the single-scale cutoff in the definition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g^{(h+1)}$$\end{document}g(h+1).

We write:4.109\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \sum _{\theta \in {\mathscr {T}}_{n;s, \omega ,\omega }^{(h+1)}} \text {d}_{\nu } \theta ({\boldsymbol{0}}_{\beta }) = \sum _{j=2}^{s-1}\sum _{\begin{array}{c} \theta \in {\mathscr {T}}_{n;s, \omega ,\omega }^{(h+1)} \\ v_{*} = j \end{array}} \text {d}_{\nu }\theta ({\boldsymbol{0}}_{\beta }), \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑θ∈Tn;s,ω,ω(h+1)dνθ(0β)=∑j=2s-1∑θ∈Tn;s,ω,ω(h+1)v∗=jdνθ(0β),and we further split:4.110\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} Using the bound (4.84), we easily get:4.111\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} | \text {B} | \le C s! |\lambda |^{s} e^{-\frac{{\tilde{c}}}{4} L_{2}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|B|≤Cs!|λ|se-c~4L2.Observe that s\ge 2s≥2, since there must be at least two off-diagonal vertices. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{v\le v_{*}} n_{v} \ne 0$$\end{document}∑v≤v∗nv≠0 (otherwise the propagator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g^{(h+1)}$$\end{document}g(h+1) connecting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{*}$$\end{document}v∗ to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{*} + 1$$\end{document}v∗+1 is vanishing), by the Diophantine condition (2.9) we have:4.112\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Big | \sum _{v\le v_{*}} n_{v} \Big | \ge K\gamma ^{-h / \tau }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|∑v≤v∗nv|≥Kγ-h/τ.This will introduce an extra small factor in the estimate for the beta function. To see this, we estimate the first product in Eq. (4.84) as:4.113\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Big ( \prod _{v\in \mathscr {N}(\theta )} e^{-\frac{c}{2}|n_{v}|} \Big )\le & \Big ( \prod _{\begin{array}{c} v\in \mathscr {N}(\theta ) \\ v\le v_{*} \end{array}} e^{-\frac{c}{4}|n_{v}|} \Big ) \Big ( \prod _{v\in \mathscr {N}(\theta )} e^{-\frac{c}{4}|n_{v}|} \Big ) \nonumber \\\le & e^{-\frac{c}{4} K \gamma ^{-h/\tau }} \Big ( \prod _{v\in \mathscr {N}(\theta )} e^{-\frac{c}{4}|n_{v}|} \Big ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(∏v∈N(θ)e-c2|nv|)≤(∏v∈N(θ)v≤v∗e-c4|nv|)(∏v∈N(θ)e-c4|nv|)≤e-c4Kγ-h/τ(∏v∈N(θ)e-c4|nv|),where the second bound follows from (4.112). If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Big |\sum _{v\le v_{*}} n_{v}\Big | > L_{1} / 2$$\end{document}|∑v≤v∗nv|>L1/2 we proceed as after (4.107), and also in this case we end up with the estimate (4.113). We then get:4.114\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |\text {A}| \le Cs! |\lambda |^{s}e^{-\frac{c}{4} K \gamma ^{-h/\tau }} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|A|≤Cs!|λ|se-c4Kγ-h/τAlso here s\ge 2s≥2, since there must be at least two non-resonant vertices. We are now ready to prove the estimate for the beta function. Putting together (4.110), (4.111), (4.114) we obtain, recalling that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{2} \ge C\beta \ge C\gamma ^{-h}$$\end{document}L2≥Cβ≥Cγ-h, and using that s\ge 2s≥2:4.115\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} | \beta ^{v}_{\nu , \omega ,h} | \le K C_{m}|\lambda |^{2} \gamma ^{m h},\qquad \forall m\in \mathbb {N}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|βν,ω,hv|≤KCm|λ|2γmh,∀m∈N.This concludes the proof of Proposition 4.9. □ □

The control of the flow of the running coupling constants completes the construction of the effective potential on all scales, and concludes the proof of Proposition 4.2. To conclude the section, we observe that the effective potential admits improved estimates.

Proposition 4.10

(Improved bound for the effective potential). There exists 0<ξ < 10<ξ<1 such that:4.116\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} | \text {d}_{0}^{n_{0}} \text {d}_{1}^{n_{1}} V^{(h)}_{n;\omega ,\omega '}({\boldsymbol{q}})| \le C_{n_{0},n_{1}}|\lambda | \gamma ^{\xi h}\gamma ^{h(1 - n_{0} - n_{1})} e^{-\frac{c}{4} |n|} e^{- \frac{{\tilde{c}}}{4} \delta _{\omega ,-\omega '} L_{2}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|d00d11Vn;ω,ω′(h)(q)|≤Cn0,n1|λ|γξhγh(1-n0-n1)e-c4|n|e-c~4δω,-ω′L2.

Remark 4.11

That is, the bound (4.116) improves the estimate (4.52) by a gain factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ^{\xi h}$$\end{document}γξh.

Proof

The proof is based on a simple adaptation of the chain expansion discussed above; let us sketch it. Consider the graphs with only resonant nodes (that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_{v} = 0$$\end{document}nv=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _{v} = \omega _{v}'$$\end{document}ωv=ωv′), and recall the bound \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\nu _{h,\omega }| \le C|\lambda | \gamma ^{\xi h}$$\end{document}|νh,ω|≤C|λ|γξh. The factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ^{\xi h}$$\end{document}γξh in the estimate for the running coupling constants allows us to obtain the desired dimensional gain in the estimate for the fully resonant graphs. Consider now the graphs with at least one non-resonant node (there has to be at least one non-resonant node if n≠ 0n≠0). Non-resonant nodes are on scale 0: thus, the cluster stucture of the graphs contributing to the effective potential reaches scale 0. For this reason, we can rewrite the last factor in (4.84) as, for 0<ξ <10<ξ<1:4.117\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \prod _{T\in \mathscr {C}(\theta ) \cup \mathscr {N}_{\text {nr}}(\theta )} \gamma ^{ (h^{\text {ext}}_{T} - h_{T})}&= \Big (\prod _{T\in \mathscr {C}(\theta ) \cup \mathscr {N}_{\text {nr}}(\theta )} \gamma ^{ \xi (h^{\text {ext}}_{T} - h_{T})}\Big ) \Big ( \prod _{T\in \mathscr {C}(\theta ) \cup \mathscr {N}_{\text {nr}}(\theta )} \gamma ^{ (1-\xi )(h^{\text {ext}}_{T} - h_{T})} \Big ) \\&\le \gamma ^{\xi h} \Big ( \prod _{T\in \mathscr {C}(\theta ) \cup \mathscr {N}_{\text {nr}}(\theta )} \gamma ^{ (1-\xi )(h^{\text {ext}}_{T} - h_{T})} \Big ), \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∏T∈C(θ)∪Nnr(θ)γ(hText-hT)=(∏T∈C(θ)∪Nnr(θ)γξ(hText-hT))(∏T∈C(θ)∪Nnr(θ)γ(1-ξ)(hText-hT))≤γξh(∏T∈C(θ)∪Nnr(θ)γ(1-ξ)(hText-hT)),where we used that the external scale of the deepest cluster is h and the internal scale of the highest cluster is 0, and we estimated the first factor as a telescopic product. This allows us to extract a factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ^{\xi h}$$\end{document}γξh also for the graphs containing at least one non-resonant node; the remaining product allows us to control the sum over the scale labels, since 1-ξ >01-ξ>0. Finally, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega \ne \omega '$$\end{document}ω≠ω′ the graph must have at least one off-diagonal node; the exponential decay in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{2}$$\end{document}L2 in (4.116) follows from (4.84). This concludes the proof. □ □

Our next task will be to compute the two-point correlation function of the model; this will be discussed in the next section, via an adaptation of the multi-scale analysis used to construct the effective potential.

The Two-point Function

In this section we shall discuss how to adapt the previous multiscale analysis, to compute the two point correlation function (2.24). Being the state quasi-free, the computation of the two-point correlation function allows us to determine all correlation functions of the model via Wick’s rule.

Remark 4.12

Remark 4.14

These bounds also allow to control the kernels \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{(h-1)}_{\psi \phi }$$\end{document}Wψϕ(h-1), using that:4.138\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} W^{(h-1)}_{\psi \phi ;n,\omega ,\sigma } ( (q_{0}, q_{1}), x_{2}) = \overline{W^{(h-1)}_{\phi \psi ;-n,\omega ,\sigma } \big ( (-q_{0}, q_{1} + n\alpha ), x_{2}\big )}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Wψϕ;n,ω,σ(h-1)((q0,q1),x2)=Wϕψ;-n,ω,σ(h-1)((-q0,q1+nα),x2)¯.

This identity is proved as (4.53), see Sect. 4.2.4.

Proof

Let us start by proving (4.135). To begin, recall the bound (4.116), for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{2} \ge C\beta $$\end{document}L2≥Cβ:4.139\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big | V^{(h-1)}_{n-m;\omega ',\omega }({\boldsymbol{q}} + m {\boldsymbol{\alpha }})\big | \le C \gamma ^{h(1+\xi )} e^{-\frac{c}{4} |n-m|} |\lambda | e^{-\frac{{\tilde{c}}}{4} L_{2} \delta _{\omega ,-\omega '} }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|Vn-m;ω′,ω(h-1)(q+mα)|≤Cγh(1+ξ)e-c4|n-m||λ|e-c~4L2δω,-ω′.We will proceed by induction. Let h<0h<0, and assume that:4.140\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big | W^{(h)}_{\phi \psi ;n,\omega ,\sigma } ( {\boldsymbol{q}}, x_{2}) \big | \le \sum ^{0}_{j > h} \gamma ^{\xi j} C|\lambda |^{\delta _{n\ne 0}} e^{-\frac{c}{8}|n|} e^{- \frac{{\tilde{c}}}{32}|x_{2}|_{\omega }}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|Wϕψ;n,ω,σ(h)(q,x2)|≤∑j>h0γξjC|λ|δn≠0e-c8|n|e-c~32|x2|ω.Clearly, Eq. (4.140) also implies:4.141\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big | W^{(h)}_{\phi \psi ;n,\omega ,\sigma } ( {\boldsymbol{q}}, x_{2}) \big | \le K|\lambda |^{\delta _{n\ne 0}} e^{-\frac{c}{8}|n|} e^{- \frac{\tilde{c}}{32}|x_{2}|_{\omega }}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|Wϕψ;n,ω,σ(h)(q,x2)|≤K|λ|δn≠0e-c8|n|e-c~32|x2|ω,which is (4.135). The assumption (4.140) is true for h=0h=0, by (4.125). Our plan will be to check (4.140), using the recursion relation (4.134). Remark 4.15

The bound (4.137) allows us to prove that, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert {\boldsymbol{q}} + n{\boldsymbol{\alpha }}\Vert \le \gamma ^{h}$$\end{document}‖q+nα‖≤γh:4.152\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\big | W^{(h-1)}_{\phi \psi ;n,\omega ,\sigma } ( {\boldsymbol{q}}, x_{2}) - W^{(h-1)}_{\phi \psi ;n,\omega ,\sigma } ( -n{\boldsymbol{\alpha }}, x_{2}) \big |\\&\quad \le \int _{0}^{1} dt\, \Big |\frac{d}{dt} W^{(h-1)}_{\phi \psi ;n,\omega ,\sigma } \big ( -n{\boldsymbol{\alpha }} + t ({\boldsymbol{q}} + n{\boldsymbol{\alpha }}), x_{2}\big )\Big | \\&\quad \le C \gamma ^{\xi h} |\lambda |^{\delta _{n\ne 0}} e^{-\frac{c}{8}|n|} e^{- \frac{{\tilde{c}}}{32}|x_{2}|_{\omega }}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|Wϕψ;n,ω,σ(h-1)(q,x2)-Wϕψ;n,ω,σ(h-1)(-nα,x2)|≤∫01dt|ddtWϕψ;n,ω,σ(h-1)(-nα+t(q+nα),x2)|≤Cγξh|λ|δn≠0e-c8|n|e-c~32|x2|ω.This will be particularly useful in the analysis of the two-point function.

Proof of Theorem 3.1

Discussion of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{B}^{(h)}$$\end{document}B(h). Since the single scale propagator is bounded dimensionally as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ^{-h}$$\end{document}γ-h, while the potential term is bounded proportionally to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ^{h(1+\xi )}$$\end{document}γh(1+ξ), we have a net dimensional gain of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ^{\xi h}$$\end{document}γξh with respect to the leading term in (4.172).
